Compared to the Creator’s plans, all the plans of even the most powerful humans are nothing (see [Ps 2:2–5](https://ref.ly/Ps2:2-Ps2:5)). They are like dust that can be blown away with one puff of his breath (see [Isa 40:15–17](https://ref.ly/Isa40:15-Isa40:17)).

The gods of the pagan nations are not in the same category as the Lord. They do not deserve to be called holy (see study note on 3:5).

## Exodus 15:13 (id: 11065)

In light of God’s unfailing love and his might, there is no reason to doubt that he will be able to keep his promises. “Unfailing love” is a translation of the Hebrew word khesed*,* which speaks of the undeserved kindness and loyalty of a superior to an inferior. It is the most frequent descriptor of God’s character in the Old Testament (see [Ps 136](https://ref.ly/Ps136:1-Ps136:26)). If God had such a character but did not have the might to carry out his good intentions toward his people, his character would be of little value to us. The good news is that he has both a loving character and all power. • sacred home: God has chosen his people and gathered them to himself in order to dwell in their midst (see also [Exod 15:17](https://ref.ly/Exod15:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Redeem (ID: `keyterm:Redeem`)
## Exodus 15:13–18 (id: 11064)

The people God has redeemed have confidence for the future. In light of what the Lord had done in rescuing Israel from his enemies, there was no question that he would be able to carry through on his promise to take them safely into the Promised Land.

Philistia, Edom*,* Moab*,* and Canaan are the four peoples that would most feel the hand of God’s judgment as he dispossessed them to give the land to the people of Israel. ## Exodus 16:3 (id: 11089)

*ate our fill of bread:* The Israelites had been oppressed slaves in Egypt. Their complaint did not match the reality of their suffering.

One danger of complaining is that it can blind people to the truth. It can make the past seem better than it really was.

Faith responds differently. Faith is thankful for what God has already done and trusts him for what he will do in the future.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 16:3

## Exodus 16:4–5 (id: 11090)

These are the Lord’s instructions for gathering the food that he would provide in the wilderness. Enough was provided for each day, with a double amount provided on the sixth day so that the people would not have to gather any on the Sabbath (see [16:21–30](https://ref.ly/Exod16:21-Exod16:30)). The Israelites thus observed the Sabbath even before it was codified in the Decalogue. We instinctively resist a style of life in which it is necessary to depend on God each day to supply our needs. We wish to have supplies in advance so that we can feel independent. God was training the people for a life of faith (cp. [Matt 6:11](https://ref.ly/Matt6:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 16:4-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Exodus 16:7 (id: 11091)

The Hebrew word translated glory connotes weightiness, substance, and reality. It is not the flimsy, ephemeral quality we often associate with the English term *glory.* It is more the idea of royal grandeur.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 16:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Exodus 16:15 (id: 11083)

The Hebrew man hu’ (What is it?) came to be the name of the miraculous food (“manna,” see [16:31](https://ref.ly/Exod16:31)). For forty years, the people ate *what is it?* Jesus referred to himself as the fulfillment of the meaning of this miracle. He was the “true bread from heaven” that gives life ([John 6:32–35](https://ref.ly/John6:32-John6:35), [48](https://ref.ly/John6:48), [51](https://ref.ly/John6:51), [63](https://ref.ly/John6:63)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 16:15

## Exodus 16:21–30 (id: 11085)

Although the Sabbath was a day set apart for the Lord ([16:23](https://ref.ly/Exod16:23)), it was also the Lord’s gift to his people ([16:29](https://ref.ly/Exod16:29)). Rest and worship are not meant to be an obligation, but a privilege. However, given the human determination to meet our needs in our own way, rest and worship are given as commands ([16:28](https://ref.ly/Exod16:28)).

## Exodus 17:2 (id: 11096)

testing the Lord is explained in [17:7](https://ref.ly/Exod17:7). They doubted that God was really with them or cared for them, and they demanded that he prove his presence and care. God invites a test based on faith (“I do believe, but help me overcome my unbelief,” [Mark 9:24](https://ref.ly/Mark9:24)), but he abhors a test based on doubt (i.e., *I don’t believe, and I think God should prove himself to me,* as in [John 6:30](https://ref.ly/John6:30)). The test based on doubt makes us the judge and God the defendant.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 17:2

## Exodus 17:8 (id: 11100)

Amalek was the grandson of Esau ([Genesis 36:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen36:11-Gen36:12)). Amalek’s descendants were a nomadic people. This means they moved from place to place instead of living in one city.

They were loosely based in the land of Edom. This means Edom was their main area, even though they traveled often. They likely supported themselves by attacking and robbing nearby settled communities.

## Exodus 18:13–27 (id: 11105)

Jethro’s wise advice shows another example of God’s care and guidance. This time, God did not act through a miracle. Instead, He used practical wisdom.

Jethro taught Moses a new way to lead. He told Moses to share his authority with other capable leaders. This way of leadership is called delegation. It means giving responsibility to others.

Before this, Moses seems to have followed an Egyptian style of leadership. That system was very strict and top\-down. All authority came from the pharaoh, who made decisions by his own command. No Egyptian law code from that time has been found.

Jethro suggested a different structure. By appointing leaders over groups of people, the work of Moses would become easier and more orderly.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 18:13-27

## Exodus 19:10–15 (id: 11110)

* In the second stage of preparation, the people received commands that helped them get ready for God’s covenant.

    First, they had to wash their clothing ([19:10](https://ref.ly/Exod19:10), [14](https://ref.ly/Exod19:14)). This act showed that God is pure.

    Second, they had to set up a boundary around the mountain and not cross it ([19:12–13](https://ref.ly/Exod19:12-Exod19:13)). This command showed that God is holy (completely set apart).

    Third, they had to abstain from sexual relations ([19:15](https://ref.ly/Exod19:15)). This instruction reminded them that God is not a sexual being and that his blessings do not come from sexual activity.

    These actions were not sinful in themselves. Under normal conditions, they were good and natural. However, the people had to set them aside for a special purpose.

    They needed to focus completely on the Lord, their King, who was about to appear before them.

    Only their obedience to the boundary command is clearly reported ([19:23](https://ref.ly/Exod19:23)). Even so, since no judgment from the Lord is recorded, it is reasonable to conclude that they obeyed the other commands as well.

* [Hebrews 12:18–22](https://ref.ly/Heb12:18-Heb12:22) compares this event at Mount Sinai with the believer’s experience at Mount Zion, which it calls “the heavenly Jerusalem.”

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 19:10-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Mount Sinai (ID: `place:SinaiMount`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)
## Exodus 19:16–25 (id: 11111)

The final phase of preparation involved phenomena that moved the people toward acceptance of the covenant. Some of the experiences were visual: lightning and a cloud ([19:16](https://ref.ly/Exod19:16)), smoke and fire ([19:18](https://ref.ly/Exod19:18)). The people also heard thunder*,* a ram’s horn*,* and the voice of God ([19:16](https://ref.ly/Exod19:16), [19](https://ref.ly/Exod19:19)), and the whole mountain shook violently ([19:18](https://ref.ly/Exod19:18)). In the Bible, such phenomena are often associated with a *theophany,* which is a visible manifestation of God’s holy presence.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 19:16-25

## Exodus 20:1–23.33 (id: 11137)

The Sinai covenant follows closely the pattern of treaties made in the ancient Near East. In those treaties, a great king made an agreement with smaller nations. The great king was called a *suzerain* (a powerful ruler). The smaller nations were called *vassals* (people or nations under his rule).

These treaties usually followed a clear structure:

1. The treaty began with an introduction. This section named the great king who was offering the covenant ([20:1](https://ref.ly/Exod20:1)).
2. Next came a historical preamble (an introduction that explains past events). This section described what had already happened between the king and the people ([20:2](https://ref.ly/Exod20:2)).

    In Exodus, God reminds Israel that he brought them out of slavery in Egypt. This shows why he has the right to make a covenant with them.

3. Then the treaty listed the stipulations (the required terms of the agreement). These explained what each side promised to do. In ancient treaties, the king promised protection from enemies and help in times of danger. The people promised loyalty and obedience.

    In Exodus, the basic terms of the covenant appear in [20:3–17](https://ref.ly/Exod20:3-Exod20:17). These verses include the Ten Commandments. The laws are explained further in chapters [21–23](https://ref.ly/Exod21:1-Exod23:33).

4. The treaty also explained where the written copy should be kept and when it should be read publicly ([24:7](https://ref.ly/Exod24:7); [25:16](https://ref.ly/Exod25:16)). This ensured that future generations would remember the covenant.
5. Ancient treaties called on the gods to witness the agreement. In Exodus, instead of calling on pagan gods, historical events and written records serve as witnesses ([24:4](https://ref.ly/Exod24:4)). This shows that Israel’s covenant was different from pagan treaties.
6. Finally, treaties listed blessings and curses. Blessings would follow obedience. Curses would follow disobedience ([23:20–33](https://ref.ly/Exod23:20-Exod23:33)). This made it clear that the covenant had real consequences.

God used the familiar political treaty form of the ancient world. However, he removed pagan religious elements that were common at that time.

Through this covenant, God invited Israel into a formal relationship with himself as their king.

The Sermon on the Mount in the New Testament parallels this section of Exodus ([Matthew 5:1–7:29](https://ref.ly/Matt5:1-Matt7:29)). The Beatitudes ([Matthew 5:3–12](https://ref.ly/Matt5:3-Matt5:12)) correspond in some ways to the Ten Commandments.

Both passages describe how God’s people should live under his rule.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:1-23:33

## Exodus 20:2 (id: 11150)

This sentence is part of the introduction in the ancient covenant format. It provides the historical context that makes the agreement between two groups possible and reasonable. 

We are told that the *LORD your God* offered the covenant. He was not an unknown god who offered this special relationship. He was the God of Israel’s ancestors who showed his power and care directly to them. This revelation was a gracious act of rescue, proving he is the only God.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)
## Exodus 20:3 (id: 11153)

Not recognizing any other god is the first step toward learning the truth that there are no other gods besides the Lord (see [Deuteronomy 4:35](https://ref.ly/Deut4:35); [6:4](https://ref.ly/Deut6:4); [Isaiah 43:10–11](https://ref.ly/Isa43:10-Isa43:11); [45:21–22](https://ref.ly/Isa45:21-Isa45:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Exodus 20:3–11 (id: 11151)

The first four commandments focus on a person’s relationship with God. These commandments teach people how to honor and worship him in the right way.

If the Israelites obeyed these commandments, they would understand who God truly is. This would help them reject false ideas about God.

In Egypt, they had seen people worship many idols (images used as gods). Later, in Canaan, they would see similar idol worship again. The first four commandments helped them stay faithful to the one true God instead of following those false beliefs.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:3-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Jealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous.2`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Pronounce Innocent (ID: `keyterm:PronounceInnocent`); Graven Image (ID: `realia:GravenImage`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)
## Exodus 20:3–17 (id: 11152)

The terms of the covenant (promises between God and his people) describe how the people were expected to behave if they were to be in a covenant with God. The first four instructions relate directly to God ([20:2–11](https://ref.ly/Exod20:2-Exod20:11)). The last six are about relationships between people ([20:12–17](https://ref.ly/Exod20:12-Exod20:17)). 

A covenant with God explains how we should treat one another. God is deeply concerned with what is right and wrong. He expects his people to show his character in all their relationships.

Other law codes in the ancient Near East did not include such strong and absolute commands. This may be because polytheism (belief in many gods) does not support one clear and final standard for everyone.

In contrast, covenants made with a human king often included absolute commands. A single king could demand complete obedience from his subjects.

In Israel’s covenant, the one King is the Creator of the universe. He alone has the full authority to declare what is right and wrong. Because of who he is, he can give absolute principles, commands, and prohibitions.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:3-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Jealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous.2`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Pronounce Innocent (ID: `keyterm:PronounceInnocent`); Graven Image (ID: `realia:GravenImage`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); House (ID: `realia:House`)
## Exodus 20:4 (id: 11154)

Refusing to make an image of God is the first step in recognizing that he is transcendent. This means God is the Creator of the universe and is completely distinct from it. God is not part of creation.

If people represent God as something in creation, they will eventually worship that created thing. Instead of worshiping the Creator, they begin to worship what God made.

This exchange leads to moral corruption and spiritual harm ([Romans 1:18–25](https://ref.ly/Rom1:18-Rom1:25)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Graven Image (ID: `realia:GravenImage`)
## Exodus 20:5–6 (id: 11155)

* *jealous God:* When Scripture calls God a jealous God, it means he strongly opposes his people turning to false gods. He does not want them to give their loyalty to other gods (see [Joshua 24:19–20](https://ref.ly/Josh24:19-Josh24:20)). God’s jealousy is not selfish. It shows his deep love and his desire for his people to remain faithful to him.
* *to the third and fourth generations … a thousand generations:* We must understand both parts of this statement together. God does not punish children for the sins of their parents. Instead, God teaches that sin has effects that can harm future generations.

    Even so, God limits the natural effects of sin to three or four generations. In contrast, he extends the effects of obedience to a thousand generations. This shows that God’s mercy is much greater than his judgment (see also [34:6–7](https://ref.ly/Exod34:6-Exod34:7); [Deuteronomy 7:9](https://ref.ly/Deut7:9)).

* *hate:* The Hebrew words translated “love” ([Exodus 20:6](https://ref.ly/Exod20:6)) and “hate” are broader than English words. They describe not only feelings but also choices.

    In the Bible, to *love* means "to choose someone or something and to act faithfully toward that choice." To *hate* means "to reject someone or something and to act in ways that show that rejection."

    Emotions and choices are connected. What we feel influences our choices, and our choices reveal what we truly feel.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:5-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous.2`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`)
## Exodus 20:7 (id: 11156)

In Hebrew thought, a person’s name is often closely connected to that person’s nature and character (see study note on 3:13–22). To misuse God's name means to make God seem unimportant or worthless. One way to do this is by swearing an oath using God's name without intending to keep it. But there are other, more serious ways to dishonor God's name (see [Numbers 20:12](https://ref.ly/Num20:12); [Amos 2:7](https://ref.ly/Amos2:7); compare [Matthew 6:9](https://ref.ly/Matt6:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:7
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 3:13-22 (ID: `11275@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pronounce Innocent (ID: `keyterm:PronounceInnocent`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Exodus 20:8–11 (id: 11157)

The Sabbath day is holy when dedicated to the Lord ([20:10](https://ref.ly/Exod20:10)). What belongs only to God shares his character. This does not mean the other six days are unholy. They are for regular work ([20:9](https://ref.ly/Exod20:9)). On this special day, we remember that our work does not meet our needs. God provides for us through his grace ([20:6](https://ref.ly/Exod20:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:8-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)
## Exodus 20:11 (id: 11138)

To be in covenant with God is to do what God does. Since God rested from his work, we must also rest ([Genesis 2:1–3](https://ref.ly/Gen2:1-Gen2:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`)
## Exodus 20:12 (id: 11140)

*honor your father and mother:* This means acknowledging that you rely on others. This is an important step toward humility. Arrogance might make you famous, but humility and gratitude lead to a long, fulfilling life. 

Both Jesus and the apostle Paul refer to this command (see [Matthew 15:4](https://ref.ly/Matt15:4); [Mark 7:10](https://ref.ly/Mark7:10); [Ephesians 6:1–3](https://ref.ly/Eph6:1-Eph6:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Exodus 20:12–17 (id: 11139)

The last six instructions focus on relationships between people. Many parts of God’s covenant teach how people should treat one another.

In Israel, right behavior toward others was closely connected to worship. Obeying God meant treating other people in ways that reflect his character.

This connection between ethics (moral behavior) and religious duty was unusual in the ancient world. Several law codes existed in the ancient Near East, and some were written before Moses. However, those laws came from human kings and were not closely connected to worship.

In contrast, the false gods of other nations were described as immoral and untrustworthy. The God of Israel is different. His people honor him by treating others as he treats them. See also [Matthew 19:17–19](https://ref.ly/Matt19:17-Matt19:19).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:12-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); House (ID: `realia:House`)
## Exodus 20:13 (id: 11141)

*murder:* This does not refer to every act of killing. The Hebrew language has another word that can mean killing in a more general sense.

The command does not forbid *all* taking of human life in every situation. Instead, it forbids the intentional killing of another person for personal reasons.

This command shows that God values each person’s physical life. Jesus later expanded this teaching in [Matthew 5:21–26](https://ref.ly/Matt5:21-Matt5:26).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:13

## Exodus 20:14 (id: 11142)

*adultery:* This includes all sexual sins. Adultery clearly shows what it means to break trust or be unfaithful.

When a person commits adultery, that person seeks sexual pleasure outside of marriage and breaks trust with a spouse.

This command teaches faithfulness in marriage and purity in sexual behavior. Jesus later expanded this teaching in [Matthew 5:27–30](https://ref.ly/Matt5:27-Matt5:30).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:14

## Exodus 20:15 (id: 11143)

Stealing shows an attitude that says, “I have the right to take what I want.” It ignores whether something belongs to someone else or whether it was earned honestly.

This command teaches the opposite attitude. It teaches respect for other people and for what belongs to them.

From God’s point of view, this command shows that he values each person. God cares not only about our lives but also about the things that belong to us.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:15

## Exodus 20:16 (id: 11144)

Choosing not to tell a lie can sometimes be a private act. A person might even feel proud about being honest.

However, this command is more specific. It forbids giving false testimony against your neighbor. This refers especially to lying in court or in a public setting where someone’s reputation or life may be at risk.

Refusing to testify falsely is an unselfish act. It shows that you care more about your neighbor’s reputation and well\-being than about protecting yourself or gaining an advantage.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:16

## Exodus 20:17 (id: 11145)

This final principle circles back to the first, for “a greedy person is an idolater” ([Col 3:5](https://ref.ly/Col3:5)). Covetousness is the worship of this world, the belief that possessions, especially those my neighbor possesses, will give me lasting happiness. To believe this is to break one’s covenant of absolute loyalty to God, who alone supplies our needs. See also [Rom 7:7](https://ref.ly/Rom7:7).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); House (ID: `realia:House`)
## Exodus 20:18–21 (id: 11146)

The people were very conscious of their sinfulness before a holy God and begged Moses to stand between them and God. Moses pointed out that God’s goal was neither to overawe them nor to destroy them. Rather, he wanted their awe of him to keep them from sinning ([20:20](https://ref.ly/Exod20:20)). Jesus is the ultimate mediator between God and people ([1 Tim 2:5](https://ref.ly/1Tim2:5)).

This interlude is about access to God. The first part ([20:18–21](https://ref.ly/Exod20:18-Exod20:21)) deals with the need for a mediator between the people and God. ## Exodus 20:22–26 (id: 11149)

The Lord is transcendent, as shown by his speaking to the people in a disembodied voice from heaven ([20:22](https://ref.ly/Exod20:22)). This would have two effects upon their practice: They were not to use silver or gold to make gods in human form (idols*,* [20:23](https://ref.ly/Exod20:23)), and they were not to carve stones for an altar ([20:25](https://ref.ly/Exod20:25)). Perhaps this is because they would be tempted to carve stone gods in the process. The altar was to be made of earth ([20:24](https://ref.ly/Exod20:24)) and uncut stones ([20:25](https://ref.ly/Exod20:25)). • The prohibition against revealing one’s nakedness ([20:26](https://ref.ly/Exod20:26)) might have been to separate Israelite religious practice from the nature worship around them. Sexual activity was an integral part of many Canaanite rituals.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:22-26

## Exodus 21:1–11 (id: 11158)

Slavery was a fact of life in the ancient world. In some cases, it was the only resort for those who were destitute. In much of the ancient world, people who were slaves felt that the gods had abandoned them. But the Lord cares about those who are in this helpless condition, so his covenant people must care also. Ultimately, the revelation that Jesus Christ had died for all people everywhere would make the practice of slavery untenable ([Gal 3:28](https://ref.ly/Gal3:28)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:1-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Redeem (ID: `keyterm:Redeem`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Hebrews (ID: `group:Hebrews`); Awl (ID: `realia:Awl`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Doorpost (ID: `realia:Doorpost`)
## Exodus 21:1–23.19 (id: 11159)

The statements found here are in the typical case\-law format of ancient Near Eastern law codes. This format begins with a hypothetical situation introduced by the word “if.” The following statement of what is to be done in such a case is introduced by “then.” While some cases are grouped into categories in these chapters, there is little attempt to separate personal, civil, or ceremonial laws from one another. For God, life is not compartmentalized. All of life is lived in relationship to God, so all kinds of behavior signify whether we are in loving submission to him or in defiance of him.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:1-23:19

## Exodus 21:2 (id: 11167)

A man might sell himself into slavery in order to get money to pay his debts. This law states that the man was never to become the permanent property of the master.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:2

## Exodus 21:3 (id: 11169)

If a man and a wife went into slavery together, they were also to go free together.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:3

## Exodus 21:4–6 (id: 11170)

The master was not required to provide a slave with a wife who would then be freed with him. The slave in such a situation had to either abandon the family he began while he was a slave or become a permanent slave.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:4-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Awl (ID: `realia:Awl`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Doorpost (ID: `realia:Doorpost`)
## Exodus 21:7–9 (id: 11171)

A female slave was treated differently from a male slave. She may have been sold with the expectation that she would become a concubine (a secondary wife). Releasing her after seven years would not necessarily protect her.

If she were sent away, she might have to return to her father. Her father had already sold her, which suggests he did not want her. Marriage would also be unlikely for a former concubine. She might even be forced into prostitution. For this reason, it was often considered better for her to remain in her master’s household.

Sometimes a man bought a girl to become his son’s wife ([21:9](https://ref.ly/Exod21:9)). In that case, the payment was not the price of a slave. It was similar to a bride\-price (a gift given to a bride’s family at marriage). In the ancient Near East, a young man usually gave money or valuable gifts to the bride’s father (see [22:16](https://ref.ly/Exod22:16); [Genesis 24:53](https://ref.ly/Gen24:53)). A woman for whom a bride\-price had been paid was not considered a slave.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:7-9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Redeem (ID: `keyterm:Redeem`)
## Exodus 21:12–17 (id: 11161)

The crimes of murder, kidnapping, and cursing or striking one’s parents were punished by death. A person who committed murder gave up his own right to live. Human life is very precious in God’s sight.

Even when a death was accidental, it could not simply be ignored. However, the law made a difference between murder and accidental killing.

God provided places of refuge (see [Numbers 35:6–28\).](https://ref.ly/Num35:6-Num35:28) These were special towns where a person who killed someone by accident could flee. There, the family of the dead person could not take revenge (see also study notes on Numbers 35:6–34).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:12-17
* **Associated Articles:** Numbers 35:6-34 (ID: `22965@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

These laws governed compensation for injuries that did not lead to death.

## Exodus 21:22–25 (id: 11165)

so she gives birth prematurely: It appears that if a child’s birth was caused prematurely and the child died (i.e., there was further injury), the penalty for murder was to be enacted. The law of retaliation (the *lex talionis*) called for a penalty that matched the injury inflicted on a victim. But this law also served to limit the punishment so it was not more severe than the original injury. Cp. [Matt 5:38–39](https://ref.ly/Matt5:38-Matt5:39).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:22-25

## Exodus 21:28–32 (id: 11166)

If an animal caused a person’s death and the owner was judged to be negligent, the relatives of the deceased could demand the death penalty for both the animal and its owner. However, that penalty was not mandatory, and the relatives could elect to accept compensation ([21:30](https://ref.ly/Exod21:30)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:28-32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Exempt (ID: `keyterm:Exempt`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Shekel (ID: `realia:Shekel`)
## Exodus 21:33–22.15 (id: 11168)

In cases involving loss of property, the responsible person had to pay compensation equal to the value of what was lost. If the person had actually stolen the property, the compensation was multiplied ([22:1](https://ref.ly/Exod22:1), [4](https://ref.ly/Exod22:4), [7](https://ref.ly/Exod22:7)). Where there was a question of responsibility, the parties were to appear before God for adjudication ([22:8](https://ref.ly/Exod22:8)). The means by which the judgment was reached is not reported.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:33-22:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Pit (ID: `realia:Pit`); House (ID: `realia:House`); To Declare Guilty (ID: `keyterm:DeclareGuilty`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Grain (ID: `flora:Grain`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`)
## Exodus 22:16–31 (id: 11172)

These laws deal with different situations about social responsibility. They are not arranged in clear groups or categories.

These laws show that every part of life relates to obedience to God. Obedience to God is not limited to worship. It includes daily actions and relationships (see study note on 21:1–23:19).

## Exodus 22:18 (id: 11173)

The practices of a sorceress (a woman who uses magic or calls on spirits) reflected the pagan worldview (a way of understanding life that does not follow the true God). God was rescuing his people from this way of thinking and living.

This worldview was completely different from the biblical one. The Lord had already commanded his people not to worship other gods (see [20:3](https://ref.ly/Exod20:3); [23:13](https://ref.ly/Exod23:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 22:18

## Exodus 22:21 (id: 11175)

The Hebrews were to treat foreigners kindly because they, too, had once been foreigners (see also [23:9](https://ref.ly/Exod23:9); [Deuteronomy 10:18–19](https://ref.ly/Deut10:18-Deut10:19)). For more on this principle, see [Luke 6:31](https://ref.ly/Luke6:31).

## Exodus 22:22–23 (id: 11176)

The widow*,* the orphan*,* and the foreigner were in a helpless and economically disadvantaged position. God, by his protective stance toward these persons, demonstrated that he does not value people on the basis of their power or wealth, the standards accepted by most humans. Instead, God values people because they share his image ([Gen 1:27](https://ref.ly/Gen1:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 22:22-23

## Exodus 22:25–27 (id: 11177)

Although interest could not be charged on a loan to another Israelite, collateral, or security, could be required. However, even the security had to be handled in a humane way. The requirements of the covenant mirrored the character of God.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 22:25-27

## Exodus 23:14–17 (id: 11183)

God commanded that every man in Israel must appear before him three times each year ([23:17](https://ref.ly/Exod23:17)). They must appear at the tabernacle (also called the *Tent of Meeting*)*.* While Israel camped around the Tent of Meeting in the wilderness, this command was not difficult to obey. The people lived close to the tabernacle.

Later, after Israel settled in the promised land, many people lived far from the Tent of Meeting and later from the temple in Jerusalem. Travel became longer and more demanding.

This command was likely meant to prevent the people from building local worship centers. If each region created its own place of worship, the nation could become divided.

Local shrines could also allow pagan influences (beliefs and practices from other religions) to enter the worship of Yahweh. By gathering at one central place, the people would remain united in their worship of the one true God.

Sadly, the people did not always carry out these commands carefully (see, for example, [2 Kings 23:21–23](https://ref.ly/2Kgs23:21-2Kgs23:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 23:14-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Unleavened Bread (ID: `realia:UnleavenedBread`)

## Exodus 23:16 (id: 11185)

* They celebrated *the* *Feast of Harvest* seven weeks after Passover. This was about the end of the barley harvest and the start of the wheat harvest (mid\-May to mid\-June). In later Judaism, this feast marked the giving of the covenant at Mount Sinai. This happened 50 days after the first Passover in Egypt (see[Leviticus 23:15–21](https://ref.ly/Lev23:15-Lev23:21); [Deuteronomy 16:9–12](https://ref.ly/Deut16:9-Deut16:12)).
* They celebrated the Feast of the Ingathering on the fifteenth day of the seventh month of the Hebrew calendar (mid\-September to mid\-October). This was after the grape harvest ended. This feast remembered the time in the wilderness when God provided for his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 23:16

## Exodus 25:1–27.19 (id: 11207)

These instructions first indicate the materials the Israelites could contribute for the construction of the Tabernacle ([25:3–7](https://ref.ly/Exod25:3-Exod25:7)). The instructions then move from the center outward, beginning with the Ark of the Covenant ([25:10–22](https://ref.ly/Exod25:10-Exod25:22)) and concluding with the courtyard ([27:9–19](https://ref.ly/Exod27:9-Exod27:19)). Two items, the altar of incense in the sanctuary and the washbasin in the courtyard, are included later ([30:1–10](https://ref.ly/Exod30:1-Exod30:10), [17–21](https://ref.ly/Exod30:17-Exod30:21)), in the section relating to the priesthood, perhaps because their use was especially related to functions of the priests.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:1-9

## Exodus 25:1–40.38 (id: 11208)

Unlike most churches today, the Tent of Meeting (the tabernacle) was not mainly a place where people gathered to meet. It was first of all the dwelling place of God. It functioned like the palace of a king. God was Israel’s divine King, and the Tabernacle was his royal tent.

In this way, churches today are more similar to later Jewish synagogues, where people gathered to worship and learn (see also study note on 40:2). 

The last section of Exodus completes God’s self\-revelation to Israel. Through these events, God brought the people out of the spiritual darkness that had held them in slavery.

This section includes three main parts:

1. Instructions for building the Tent of Meeting (chapters [25–31](https://ref.ly/Exod25:1-Exod31:18)).
2. The story of the golden calf (a wrong attempt to secure God's presence, chapters [32–34](https://ref.ly/Exod32:1-Exod34:35)).
3. The report of building the Tent of Meeting (the correct way to secure God's presence, chapters [35–40](https://ref.ly/Exod35:1-Exod40:38)).

These chapters show that God’s ultimate goal in delivering Israel from slavery was to live among them.

God wanted to share his presence with his people. However, because God is holy (completely pure and set apart from sin), people could experience his presence only in the way he commanded.

They could not approach him through pagan practices (worship methods of other nations) or through human manipulation (trying to control or force a god to act).

The building of the Tent of Meeting is described here, even though some instructions in Leviticus were given earlier (see [40:1–2](https://ref.ly/Exod40:1-Exod40:2); [Leviticus 25:1](https://ref.ly/Lev25:1); [27:34](https://ref.ly/Lev27:34); [Numbers 7:1](https://ref.ly/Num7:1)). This event shows the goal of the entire exodus story: God came to live among his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:1-9
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 40:2 (ID: `11388@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Exodus 25:2 (id: 11216)

*every man whose heart compels him:* There is a plain difference between meeting needs in God's way and trying to meet them on our own. For example, Aaron forced people to contribute to making the gold calf (see [32:1–4](https://ref.ly/Exod32:1-Exod32:4)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)
## Exodus 25:8 (id: 11218)

* *a sanctuary:* For the people to have a life\-giving relationship with God, they needed to understand how completely different God was from them (see study note on 3:5).
* The purpose of this activity was so God could live among them.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:8
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 3:5 (ID: `11285@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Exodus 25:9 (id: 11219)

*according to the pattern:* We cannot choose how to approach a holy God. We must meet God on his terms and in accord with his desires and character.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:9

## Exodus 25:10–22 (id: 11209)

an Ark of acacia wood: In translating the plans for the Ark of the Covenant, the NLT continues to use the word Ark because of its traditional associations. However, *ark* is simply an archaic English word meaning “box.” The Ark was Israel’s “covenant box.” It was the most significant item in the whole complex, because it was there that God made his presence available ([25:22](https://ref.ly/Exod25:22)). The presence of the stone tablets of the covenant in the box ([25:16](https://ref.ly/Exod25:16), [21](https://ref.ly/Exod25:21)) underlined the truth that God reveals himself in the context of a mutually binding commitment between himself and his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:10-22

## Exodus 25:22 (id: 11213)

I will meet with you there: The Ark was located in the innermost part of the sanctuary ([26:33](https://ref.ly/Exod26:33)), which was in effect God’s throne room (see study note on Lev 16:2). In a comparable pagan sanctuary, one would find an idol in this location. The Tabernacle had no idol, only a box that physically represented the covenant faithfulness and grace of God. God meets his people in that context, not in the context of idolatry. An idol reflects the belief that a deity is continuous with the created order and that it can be manipulated with the right ritual. It assumes that we can secure blessings from the deity without regard to our own character or commitment. But God is completely distinct from this world, and he cannot be manipulated in any way. He wants to bless us, but those blessings can only be given to those who receive his grace as a gift and surrender to him in trust.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:22
* **Associated Articles:** Leviticus 16:2 (ID: `20161@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Exodus 25:31–39 (id: 11217)

The lampstand of pure, hammered gold stood on the south side of the Holy Place ([26:35](https://ref.ly/Exod26:35)). It showed the light of God’s presence to his people. The lamps ([25:37](https://ref.ly/Exod25:37)) were flat dishes containing olive oil. A wick extended into the oil and hung down from a lip on one side of the dish. The dishes, resting on the upturned almond blossoms ([25:33](https://ref.ly/Exod25:33)), were to be turned so that all their wicks faced the center of the room ([25:37](https://ref.ly/Exod25:37)). The total number of lamps is not clear. Although seven lamps are mentioned in [25:37](https://ref.ly/Exod25:37), there seem to be 22 lamp cups in [25:33–34](https://ref.ly/Exod25:33-Exod25:34).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:31-39

## Exodus 26:1–37 (id: 11220)

The tabernacle itself (not including the courtyard around it) was not very large. It was about 4\.6 meters (15 feet) wide and 13\.7 meters (45 feet) long.

The main structure stood on 48 vertical frames. Each frame was 4\.6 meters (15 feet) high and 68\.6 centimeters (27 inches) wide ([26:16](https://ref.ly/Exod26:16)). The frames were made of acacia wood and covered with gold ([26:15](https://ref.ly/Exod26:15), [29](https://ref.ly/Exod26:29)). 

Workers joined the frames together with horizontal crossbars. These bars formed a three\-sided rectangle with one open end ([26:26–28](https://ref.ly/Exod26:26-Exod26:28)). 

Two large linen curtains covered the structure. Each large curtain was made from five smaller curtains joined together. The two large curtains were then connected into one long covering ([26:1–6](https://ref.ly/Exod26:1-Exod26:6)). 

This linen covering stretched across the top of the frame. It hung down along the sides and the back. It covered both the walls and the roof.

Over the linen curtain, workers placed another curtain made of goat hair. It was built in the same way. This curtain was 0\.9 meters (3 feet) wider and 1\.8 meters (6 feet) longer than the linen curtain ([26:7–13](https://ref.ly/Exod26:7-Exod26:13)). Because it was larger, it hung lower on every side.

Above these two curtains were two protective layers. One layer was made from tanned ram skins. The other was made from fine leather ([26:14](https://ref.ly/Exod26:14)). These outer coverings protected the tabernacle from weather and damage.

Inside the tabernacle, another curtain divided the space into two parts. This curtain was beautifully embroidered. It hung across the width of the structure on four posts made of acacia wood ([26:31–33](https://ref.ly/Exod26:31-Exod26:33)). This created two rooms. The first room was called the Holy Place. The second room was called the Most Holy Place.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 26:1-37

## Exodus 26:2–6 (id: 11223)

The cloth was 13 meters long (42 feet) and two meters wide (six feet). The width of two meters (six feet) may have been because of the largest loom available. They joined five of these six\-foot strips, probably by sewing, to create one continuous piece of cloth 13 meters long (42 feet) and 10 meters wide (30 feet). When connected with loops and clasps, the two cloths formed a single unit measuring 13 meters (42 feet) by 20 meters (60 feet) ([26:5–6](https://ref.ly/Exod26:5-Exod26:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 26:2-6

## Exodus 27:2 (id: 11235)

Archaeology shows that the horns of the altar were raised parts at the four corners of the altar. They projected upward from each corner.

These horns may have helped keep the wood and offerings from falling off the altar (see [Psalm 118:27](https://ref.ly/Ps118:27)). They also had meaning related to beliefs about God. The horns represented God’s gracious care for his people.

If a person held onto the horns of the altar, that person was asking for mercy in judgment (see [1 Kings 1:50–51](https://ref.ly/1Kgs1:50-1Kgs1:51); [2:28](https://ref.ly/1Kgs2:28)). This action showed a request for protection or leniency.

The priests put blood on the horns during atonement and purification ceremonies (special rituals to deal with sin and uncleanness; see [Exodus 29:12](https://ref.ly/Exod29:12); [Leviticus 4:7](https://ref.ly/Lev4:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 27:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Horns of Altar (ID: `realia:HornsOfAltar`)

The first thing said about the function of the priests, Aaron and his sons*,* is that they were to keep the lamps burning continually through the night (see [30:8](https://ref.ly/Exod30:8)). ## Exodus 27:20–30.38 (id: 11233)

After explaining how to build the Tent of Meeting, Moses gave instructions for those serving in it and the items used. Included are:

* the priests' duties ([27:20–21](https://ref.ly/Exod27:20-Exod27:21); [29:38–46](https://ref.ly/Exod29:38-Exod29:46)),
* the priests' clothing (chapter [28](https://ref.ly/Exod28:1-Exod28:43)),
* the ceremonies to set the priests apart for service ([29:1–37](https://ref.ly/Exod29:1-Exod29:37)),
* the tabernacle furnishings ([30:1–10](https://ref.ly/Exod30:1-Exod30:10), [17–21](https://ref.ly/Exod30:17-Exod30:21)), and
* the supplies used for worship ([30:11–16](https://ref.ly/Exod30:11-Exod30:16), [22–38](https://ref.ly/Exod30:22-Exod30:38)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 27:20-28:5

## Exodus 27:21 (id: 11234)

*the Tent of Meeting:* The expression *Tent of Meeting* sometimes refers to the tabernacle tent. In these cases, *Tent of Meeting* and *tabernacle* mean the same thing.

In addition, [33:7–11](https://ref.ly/Exod33:7-Exod33:11) also mentions a temporary tent called the T*ent of Meeting*. This tent was set up outside the camp before the tabernacle was built. See study note on 40:2.

## Exodus 28:1–2 (id: 11238)

The garments described in this chapter are primarily those for Aaron, the first high priest. The clothing for Aaron’s sons, who functioned as his assistants, is described in [28:40–43](https://ref.ly/Exod28:40-Exod28:43).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Priesthood (ID: `keyterm:Priesthood`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Abihu (ID: `person:Abihu`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Nadab (ID: `person:Nadab`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Eleazar (ID: `person:Eleazar.2`); Ithamar (ID: `person:Ithamar`)
## Exodus 28:1–43 (id: 11239)

These glorious and beautiful ([28:2](https://ref.ly/Exod28:2)) garments for Aaron were to distinguish him as a priest set apart (Hebrew qadash*,* “consecrated”) for \[the Lord’s] service ([28:3](https://ref.ly/Exod28:3)). The magnificence of these garments is in keeping with the grandeur of the Tabernacle, which functioned as a portable earthly palace for God. There would come a day when Jesus Christ, the true High Priest, would enter the Most Holy Place once for all, and the distinction between priests and people would fade away ([Heb 10:11–22](https://ref.ly/Heb10:11-Heb10:22)). Until that time, human mediators were needed to symbolize the wonderful thing that God was planning to do in the hearts, lives, and behaviors of those with whom he shared his presence.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:1-43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Breast-Piece (ID: `realia:Breast-piece`); Ephod (ID: `realia:Ephod`); Purple Cloth (ID: `realia:PurpleCloth`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`); Priesthood (ID: `keyterm:Priesthood`); Turban (ID: `realia:Turban`); Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`); Craftsman (ID: `realia:Craftsman`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Holy Place (ID: `place:HolyPlace`); Sash (ID: `realia:Sash`); Robe (ID: `realia:Robe`); Tunic (ID: `realia:Tunic`); Spindle (ID: `realia:Spindle`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Pomegranate (ID: `flora:Pomegranate`); Train (ID: `realia:Train`); Thread (ID: `realia:Thread`); Measuring Reed (ID: `realia:MeasuringReed`); Jinglet (ID: `realia:Jinglet`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Eleazar (ID: `person:Eleazar.2`); Ithamar (ID: `person:Ithamar`); Smear (ID: `keyterm:Smear`); Abihu (ID: `person:Abihu`); Delight (ID: `keyterm:Delight`); Nadab (ID: `person:Nadab`); Span (ID: `realia:Span`); Plate (ID: `realia:Plate`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`); Tent of Meeting (ID: `realia:TentOfMeeting`); Embroidered Cloth (ID: `realia:EmbroideredCloth`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Breeches (ID: `realia:Breeches`)
## Exodus 28:6–14 (id: 11248)

The ephod was a kind of apron consisting of front and back pieces joined by shoulder straps ([28:7](https://ref.ly/Exod28:7)) and secured by a decorative sash ([28:8](https://ref.ly/Exod28:8), [27–28](https://ref.ly/Exod28:27-Exod28:28)). Perhaps it is mentioned first because it was understood to be the distinctive high priestly garment. The fact that Gideon later made a gold ephod ([Judg 8:27](https://ref.ly/Judg8:27)) suggests that the garment was thought to have special powers.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:6-14

names on his shoulders (cp. [Isa 9:6](https://ref.ly/Isa9:6)): The badge of office on the shoulder indicated what the person was really carrying. Thus, the high priest represented the people before God, and with his royal garments he represented the glory of God before the people.

## Exodus 28:15–30 (id: 11241)

More attention is given to the chestpiece than to any other item, suggesting the importance attached to it. It apparently symbolized the priest’s representation of Israel ([28:17–21](https://ref.ly/Exod28:17-Exod28:21), [29](https://ref.ly/Exod28:29)) and was a container for the Urim and Thummim*,* by which God made his will known ([28:30](https://ref.ly/Exod28:30)). The chestpiece represented the totality of the high priest’s tasks of bringing the people to God and bringing God’s word to the people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:15-30

## Exodus 28:30 (id: 11243)

The Urim and Thummim (Hebrew terms that might translate as *lights* \[or *curses*] *and perfections*) were apparently a pair of stones that were thrown down to get a yes or no answer from God. No physical description of these objects exists; their use is further explained in [Num 27:21](https://ref.ly/Num27:21); [Deut 33:8](https://ref.ly/Deut33:8); [1 Sam 28:6](https://ref.ly/1Sam28:6); [Ezra 2:63](https://ref.ly/Ezra2:63); and [Neh 7:65](https://ref.ly/Neh7:65).

## Exodus 28:31–35 (id: 11244)

The robe worn under the ephod is described. The bells signaled that the priest was still moving and had not been struck dead by God’s presence (see [Lev 10:1–2](https://ref.ly/Lev10:1-Lev10:2)). Jewish tradition tells us that the priest had a cord tied to his ankle so that if he died in the Holy Place ([Exod 28:35](https://ref.ly/Exod28:35)), his body could be dragged out.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:31-35

## Exodus 28:36–38 (id: 11245)

Like all other parts of the high priest's clothing and actions, the little gold plate on the front of his turban reminded the people that God is holy. His holiness affected how they could enter his presence. God wanted to live with his people, but his holiness would harm them unless he took protective measures. God decides how we can enter his presence, not us. 

The Bible's main question is how sinful people can live with a holy God so that God can share his holy character with them. Jesus Christ, the Holy One of God, provides the answer. He is the perfect mediator ([Luke 4:34](https://ref.ly/Luke4:34); [Hebrews 9:11–15](https://ref.ly/Heb9:11-Heb9:15)). He did not die only to save us from the consequences of our sins. He died so that God can live in us and create his holy character in us ([Ephesians 2:10](https://ref.ly/Eph2:10); [4:22](https://ref.ly/Eph4:22); [Philippians 1:27](https://ref.ly/Phil1:27); [1 Peter 1:13–16](https://ref.ly/1Pet1:13-1Pet1:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:36-38

## Exodus 28:42–43 (id: 11247)

In many pagan religions, sexual acts were part of worship. People believed they could control fertility (the ability to have children, crops, and animals) through sexual activity.

In some places, priests even served their gods without clothing. These practices were attempts to influence the powers of nature.

In the worship of the Lord, no form of sexual manipulation was allowed. God’s blessings cannot be gained through magical actions or human control.

For this reason, even the accidental exposure of the genitals was carefully avoided. Worship of the Lord had to reflect his holiness and purity.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:42-43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy Place (ID: `place:HolyPlace`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`); Breeches (ID: `realia:Breeches`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`); Tent of Meeting (ID: `realia:TentOfMeeting`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`)

## Exodus 29:1–37 (id: 11249)

Moses was required to consecrate (or *sanctify*) Aaron and his sons to serve the Lord. This emphasis on making the priests holy is found throughout the ceremonies ([29:6](https://ref.ly/Exod29:6), [21](https://ref.ly/Exod29:21), [28](https://ref.ly/Exod29:28), [29](https://ref.ly/Exod29:29), [34](https://ref.ly/Exod29:34), [36](https://ref.ly/Exod29:36), [37](https://ref.ly/Exod29:37)). They were set apart not merely for service, but to serve a God whose nature is utterly different from that of fallen, sinful humans. The report of how these instructions were carried out is found in [Lev 8](https://ref.ly/Lev8:1-Lev8:36).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 29:1-37

*wash them with water:* This action showed an important truth. No one can live in God’s presence while carrying the filth of sin in their lives ([29:21](https://ref.ly/Exod29:21)).

The washing did not remove sin by itself. It was a sign. It showed that people must be made clean before coming near to God.

## Exodus 29:9 (id: 11268)

forever: The fulfillment of this promise, as with the one to David, clearly leads beyond the merely human. Aaron’s own behavior was less than sterling (e.g., ch [32](https://ref.ly/Exod32:1-Exod32:35); [Num 11](https://ref.ly/Num11:1-Num11:35)), and the behavior of his sons and descendants was no better ([1 Sam 2:12–17](https://ref.ly/1Sam2:12-1Sam2:17); [Isa 28:7](https://ref.ly/Isa28:7); [Ezek 8:15–16](https://ref.ly/Ezek8:15-Ezek8:16); [Mal 2:1–9](https://ref.ly/Mal2:1-Mal2:9)). The promise could only find its ultimate fulfillment in Jesus Christ (see [Heb 7:26](https://ref.ly/Heb7:26); [9:11](https://ref.ly/Heb9:11)). • To ordain (literally *to fill the hands*) means to give a person a task to perform.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 29:9

## Exodus 29:10–34 (id: 11250)

The consecration of the priesthood of Aaron involved a sin offering ([29:10–14](https://ref.ly/Exod29:10-Exod29:14)), a burnt offering ([29:15–18](https://ref.ly/Exod29:15-Exod29:18)), and an ordination offering ([29:19–28](https://ref.ly/Exod29:19-Exod29:28)). The same patterns established here are expanded to the regular offerings of the people (see [29:38–46](https://ref.ly/Exod29:38-Exod29:46); [Lev 1–5](https://ref.ly/Lev1:1-Lev5:19)). In all three cases, Aaron and his sons lay their hands on \[the] head of the sacrificial animals ([Exod 29:10](https://ref.ly/Exod29:10), [15](https://ref.ly/Exod29:15), [19](https://ref.ly/Exod29:19)), indicating that sin is a matter of life and death, and that it can only be removed by a death. Because it represents life, blood is prominent in these ceremonies ([29:12](https://ref.ly/Exod29:12), [16](https://ref.ly/Exod29:16), [20](https://ref.ly/Exod29:20), [21](https://ref.ly/Exod29:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 29:10-34

## Exodus 29:19–28 (id: 11255)

This ordination offering is very similar to the peace (or fellowship) offering later described in [Lev 3](https://ref.ly/Lev3:1-Lev3:17). The fat was burned as a gift to the Lord, but the breast and the thigh, after having been dedicated to the Lord by lifting them up before him ([29:24–26](https://ref.ly/Exod29:24-Exod29:26)), became the portion for the priests to eat ([29:26–28](https://ref.ly/Exod29:26-Exod29:28)). In the regular peace offering, the remainder of the meat was to be eaten by the person making the offering in a fellowship meal ([Lev 7:11–18](https://ref.ly/Lev7:11-Lev7:18)), as the priests did here ([Exod 29:31–34](https://ref.ly/Exod29:31-Exod29:34)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 29:19-28

## Exodus 29:33 (id: 11261)

*atonement:* Traditionally, *atonement* has often been explained as a “covering over.” This means that sin is covered so it no longer stands between God and people.

Some modern scholars connect the word to another Semitic root (a related word family from ancient Middle Eastern languages). That root does not appear elsewhere in the Hebrew Bible. Based on that idea, they understand the word to mean “blot out,” “erase,” or “cleanse.”

In both views, the result is the same. The effects of sin are removed. Because of this, people and even sacred objects can remain in God’s presence without being destroyed.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 29:33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Appease (ID: `keyterm:Appease`)
## Exodus 29:37 (id: 11262)

As with the priests, the altar was to be made holy so it could be used in service of the holy God.

outside the inner curtain: That is, in the Holy Place, just outside the Most Holy Place where the Ark was located. • The rising smoke of the incense symbolized prayer. Believers now have constant access to God in prayer through the continuous atonement provided through Christ (see [Ps 141:2](https://ref.ly/Ps141:2); [Luke 1:10](https://ref.ly/Luke1:10); [Rev 5:8](https://ref.ly/Rev5:8); [8:3–4](https://ref.ly/Rev8:3-Rev8:4)).

## Exodus 30:12 (id: 11290)

no plague: Cp. [2 Sam 24](https://ref.ly/2Sam24:1-2Sam24:25), when a plague followed upon David’s unauthorized census. Perhaps a census was often taken for reasons of pride and domination. Here the people are giving God a ransom for themselves. This standard payment would go into the Tabernacle treasury and initially be used in the construction of the Tabernacle (see [Exod 38:25–26](https://ref.ly/Exod38:25-Exod38:26)).

## Exodus 31:1–11 (id: 11295)

## Exodus 31:3 (id: 11300)

*I have filled him with the Spirit of God:* This is one of the earliest references to being filled with the Spirit as an expression of divine empowerment for activities that are clearly beyond normal human abilities (see also [Genesis 41:38](https://ref.ly/Gen41:38); [Numbers 11:17](https://ref.ly/Num11:17); [Judges 6:34](https://ref.ly/Judg6:34); [14:19](https://ref.ly/Judg14:19); [1 Samuel 10:6](https://ref.ly/1Sam10:6); [16:13](https://ref.ly/1Sam16:13); [Joel 2:28–29](https://ref.ly/Joel2:28-Joel2:29); [Micah 3:8](https://ref.ly/Mic3:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 31:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Understanding (ID: `keyterm:Understanding`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Intention (ID: `keyterm:Intention`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Exodus 31:6 (id: 11301)

God gave detailed and remarkable instructions. He also provided skilled craftsmen who could complete the work exactly as he commanded.

In this way, God supplied what his people needed to obey him. This idea is similar to a prayer by Augustine in his book *Confessions*: “Give what you command, and command what you will” (10\.29\). Augustine meant that God must give people the ability to do what he requires.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 31:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ahisamach (ID: `person:Ahisamach`); Oholiab (ID: `person:Oholiab`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Tribe of Dan (ID: `group:Dan`); Dan (ID: `place:Dan`)
## Exodus 31:11 (id: 11296)

The main idea of this whole section ([25:1–40:38](https://ref.ly/Exod25:1-Exod40:38)) is clear. God wanted to meet his people’s need for a visible sign of his presence. They needed something they could see that reminded them that he was with them.

However, this had to be done exactly as God commanded. God set the way his people were to approach him. He determines the terms by which people come to him, not human beings.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 31:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy Place (ID: `place:HolyPlace`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`)
## Exodus 31:12–18 (id: 11297)

The Sabbath was understood to be the unique sign of the covenant ([31:13](https://ref.ly/Exod31:13), [16](https://ref.ly/Exod31:16), [17](https://ref.ly/Exod31:17); see also [20:8–11](https://ref.ly/Exod20:8-Exod20:11); [Isa 58:13–14](https://ref.ly/Isa58:13-Isa58:14); [Jer 17:21–27](https://ref.ly/Jer17:21-Jer17:27); [Neh 13:15–22](https://ref.ly/Neh13:15-Neh13:22)). The Sabbath is also said to figure significantly in the achievement of God’s underlying purpose of making his people holy*,* that is, set apart for his exclusive use and sharing his character ([Exod 31:13](https://ref.ly/Exod31:13), [14](https://ref.ly/Exod31:14), [15](https://ref.ly/Exod31:15)). Resting from their own work ([31:15](https://ref.ly/Exod31:15), [17](https://ref.ly/Exod31:17)) was also a practical way of demonstrating genuine dependence on God to meet their needs (see [20:8–11](https://ref.ly/Exod20:8-Exod20:11) and study notes).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 31:12-18
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 20:8-11 (ID: `11157@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Testimony (ID: `keyterm:Witness.2`); Mount Sinai (ID: `place:SinaiMount`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Tablet (ID: `realia:Tablet`)
## Exodus 31:14–15 (id: 11298)

It is absolutely important to learn that we can find life in God only by coming to him in his way. This is underlined by the death sentence for disobedience (see also [Num 15:32–36](https://ref.ly/Num15:32-Num15:36)). This is not to say that followers of God must always punish those who commit these offenses in this way; but here at the outset, it was vital to make this truth clear (see [Acts 5:1–11](https://ref.ly/Acts5:1-Acts5:11)). If the spiritual truth of this lesson has not been learned, mere ritual obedience will be of no value (see [Col 2:16–19](https://ref.ly/Col2:16-Col2:19)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 31:14-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Exodus 31:18 (id: 11299)

Normally, each party to a covenant received a written copy of the terms of the covenant. So it may be that the two stone tablets were identical, one being God’s copy and the other the people’s copy, to be kept together in the Ark of the Covenant. • finger of God: See [34:28](https://ref.ly/Exod34:28).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 31:18

## Exodus 32:1 (id: 11310)

The Israelites’ actions were motivated by fear, disrespect for this fellow Moses*,* disbelief in God’s leadership, and denial of responsibility. They were unwilling to wait for God to reveal his plans of care for them. Refusal to wait on God is often a cause of sin (see [1 Sam 13:7–13](https://ref.ly/1Sam13:7-1Sam13:13); [Isa 30:15–18](https://ref.ly/Isa30:15-Isa30:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)
## Exodus 32:1–6 (id: 11303)

The people were not willing to wait and see what God had been saying to Moses on the mountain for the forty days while he was there (see [24:18](https://ref.ly/Exod24:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:1-6

## Exodus 32:1–35 (id: 11302)

At the foot of Mount Sinai, after Moses had been absent for many days, the people felt the need for protection, guidance, and a tangible way to express their worship. God knew this and was eager to meet these needs (chs [25–31](https://ref.ly/Exod25:1-Exod31:18)). The Israelites, however, tried to meet their needs for themselves. Fellowship with God requires depending on him (see [John 15:5](https://ref.ly/John15:5); [2 Cor 3:5](https://ref.ly/2Cor3:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:1-35

## Exodus 32:2–4 (id: 11311)

The religious professional, Aaron*,* demanded a specific contribution of gold rings and then excluded the people from any further involvement in the process. This is very different from what God had commanded regarding the construction of the Tabernacle, where the people were invited to bring many different kinds of things as they felt led ([25:1–9](https://ref.ly/Exod25:1-Exod25:9); [35:4–36:7](https://ref.ly/Exod35:4-Exod36:7)) and to share in the work under the guidance of a Spirit\-filled layperson ([31:1–6](https://ref.ly/Exod31:1-Exod31:6); [36:1–2](https://ref.ly/Exod36:1-Exod36:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:2-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Earring (ID: `realia:Earring`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Molten Image (ID: `realia:MoltenImage`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)
## Exodus 32:4 (id: 11320)

* *fashioned it into a molten calf:* The idol might have been a bull, similar to the Egyptian god Amon\-Re, which the people knew from Egypt. The bull represented power, domination, and fertility. The writer might have said calf to despise the idol. Or, Aaron might have made a calf, thinking this sin was less severe than making a full\-sized bull.
* *These, O Israel, are your gods, who brought you up out:* The people credited the idol with what they had just said Moses did ([32:1](https://ref.ly/Exod32:1)). Idolatry shows the belief that the divine and visible worlds are continuous. This view suggests one can access divine power by manipulating the god through the idol.

    God insisted the opposite is true. God's creation cannot contain or limit him. No one can receive his blessing by manipulating creation, either in a ceremony or otherwise. God's blessings are for those who give up their own efforts to feel secure and come to him in the ways he has revealed.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Molten Image (ID: `realia:MoltenImage`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)
## Exodus 32:5 (id: 11321)

* Aaron tried to manage the process, but he gave up leadership by simply doing what he thought people wanted.
* Although Aaron called the idol "your gods" ([32:4](https://ref.ly/Exod32:4)), he also suggested it was a physical manifestation of the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`)
## Exodus 32:6 (id: 11322)

The Hebrew term translated as *indulge in* *revelry* is traditionally translated as *they got up to play.* In Hebrew, like in English, the word *play* can imply sexual activities (see [Genesis 26:8](https://ref.ly/Gen26:8), “caressing”). This is likely the case here. 

Worship of a fertility symbol, such as a bull, often included the worshipers engaging in sexual activities (see [1 Corinthians 10:7–8](https://ref.ly/1Cor10:7-1Cor10:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`)
## Exodus 32:7 (id: 11323)

*your people, whom you brought up out of the land of Egypt:* HereGod attributes his own work to Moses. This suggests God was testing Moses, giving him a chance to avoid the same mistake the people made ([32:1](https://ref.ly/Exod32:1)). God was not tempting Moses to do wrong (see [James 1:13–14](https://ref.ly/Jas1:13-Jas1:14)). But he placed Moses in a situation with a clear choice that could lead in different directions (compare [1 Kings 22:19–23](https://ref.ly/1Kgs22:19-1Kgs22:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)
## Exodus 32:9–10 (id: 11324)

God was apparently ready to reject his people. They had broken their covenant (their binding agreement) with him.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:9-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Stubborn (ID: `keyterm:Stubborn`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Exodus 32:10 (id: 11304)

* *Now leave me alone:* This sounded like a command, but it was actually an invitation. God was giving Moses an opportunity to pray for the people. Moses was to stand before God as an intercessor (a person who speaks to God on behalf of others).

    The people deserved to be destroyed because of their sin. However, God was willing to spare them if Moses continued to pray for them.

* *I will make you into a great nation:* God told Moses that he could begin again with him alone. God could abandon the other descendants of Abraham and build a new nation from the children of Moses.

    This was likely a test. It showed whether Moses understood God’s character and his promises.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:10

## Exodus 32:11–13 (id: 11305)

If there was a test, Moses passed it. He refused to take God's place ([32:11](https://ref.ly/Exod32:11)). He knew God is just and faithful and would not save people just to destroy them ([32:12](https://ref.ly/Exod32:12)). He declined the offer to become the father of a great nation because it would mean God violating promises to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob ([32:13](https://ref.ly/Exod32:13)). Moses understood who God truly is.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:11-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`); Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`)
## Exodus 32:14 (id: 11306)

In response to the argument of Moses, the Lord changed his mind.

This does not show God as an angry ruler who must be forced to calm down. The Bible does not present him as a tyrant who is difficult to persuade.

Instead, the Lord is more willing to show mercy than to bring punishment. He invites those who are close to him, like Moses, to pray and speak on behalf of others.

Through faithful intercession (prayer on behalf of someone else), God gives an opportunity for mercy.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Exodus 32:15–29 (id: 11307)

When Moses saw what was happening, he became much less calm than he was on the mountain. He smashed the two tablets ([32:15–19](https://ref.ly/Exod32:15-Exod32:19)). He destroyed the calf ([32:20](https://ref.ly/Exod32:20)). He confronted Aaron ([32:21–25](https://ref.ly/Exod32:21-Exod32:25)). He ordered the death of the rebellious leaders ([32:26–29](https://ref.ly/Exod32:26-Exod32:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:15-29

## Exodus 32:16 (id: 11308)

*The tablets were the work of God:* The covenant was more than just a human agreement.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Tablet (ID: `realia:Tablet`)
## Exodus 32:19 (id: 11309)

Moses might have shattered the tablets out of anger. It could also have been his way of saying the people shattered the covenant with God. Later, God reminded Moses that he was the one who broke the tablets (see [34:1](https://ref.ly/Exod34:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Tablet (ID: `realia:Tablet`); Molten Image (ID: `realia:MoltenImage`)
## Exodus 32:20 (id: 11312)

Passing the gold powder of the image through the bodies of the people effectively rendered it unclean.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Molten Image (ID: `realia:MoltenImage`)
## Exodus 32:21–25 (id: 11313)

Aaron denied responsibility, even though he made the molds and poured the gold ([32:4](https://ref.ly/Exod32:4)). Aaron wanted Moses to believe it all just happened ([32:24](https://ref.ly/Exod32:24)). He implied he had no choice because the people were so evil ([32:22](https://ref.ly/Exod32:22)). 

Aaron did not fool Moses. Moses knew Aaron could have led the people, but instead, he allowed them to get completely out of control ([32:25](https://ref.ly/Exod32:25)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:21-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Molten Image (ID: `realia:MoltenImage`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)
## Exodus 32:25 (id: 11314)

*a laughingstock to their enemies:* After the Israelites’ great show of worshiping the Lord as a different kind of God, their actions proclaimed that he was merely another of the idols that their enemies had been worshiping all along (see [Ezekiel 36:19–20](https://ref.ly/Ezek36:19-Ezek36:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`)
## Exodus 32:26–29 (id: 11315)

Moses had asked God to spare the people, but now he called on those who followed the Lord to kill those who had sinned. The Levites ([32:26](https://ref.ly/Exod32:26)) were willing to confront the sin that Aaron had let loose. Although Moses commanded them to kill everyone ([32:27](https://ref.ly/Exod32:27)), the number 3,000 ([32:28](https://ref.ly/Exod32:28)) makes it clear that the words *each of you* had a restricted meaning. 

The reference to *his son* *and his brother* suggests that as Aaron had led in the idolatry, many of the Levites had led in the worship of the idol, and they were the ones that the rest of the Levites killed. Aaron may have escaped because God had already designated him as high priest ([28:1](https://ref.ly/Exod28:1)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:26-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Levi (ID: `person:Levi.3`); Tribe of Levi (ID: `group:Levi`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)
## Exodus 32:30–35 (id: 11316)

This further intercession for the people may have been needed because of the new recognition Moses had of how serious the sin really was.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:30-35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Scroll (ID: `realia:Scroll`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`); Appease (ID: `keyterm:Appease`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Molten Image (ID: `realia:MoltenImage`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)
## Exodus 32:32 (id: 11317)

Earlier, God had offered to make Moses into a great nation instead of the people. Moses had refused that offer.

Here, Moses again showed his loyalty to the people. He asked God to destroy him also if God chose not to forgive them.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Scroll (ID: `realia:Scroll`)
## Exodus 32:33 (id: 11318)

God did not grant what Moses requested. Instead, God said he would judge only those who had sinned in that event.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Scroll (ID: `realia:Scroll`)
## Exodus 32:34 (id: 11319)

* *the place I described:* This refers to Canaan, which God promised to give to Abraham’s descendants (see [3:17](https://ref.ly/Exod3:17); [32:13](https://ref.ly/Exod32:13); [33:1](https://ref.ly/Exod33:1)).
* *on the day I settle accounts:* Because of God’s mercy, judgment may not always come immediately. But in this instance, God soon sent a plague ([32:35](https://ref.ly/Exod32:35)). Judgment will come for those who continue to sin (see [Matthew 13:24–30](https://ref.ly/Matt13:24-Matt13:30)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 32:34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`)
## Exodus 33:1–6 (id: 11325)

God would not lead the people up to Canaan as he had previously led them (see [33:3](https://ref.ly/Exod33:3); but also [33:14–17](https://ref.ly/Exod33:14-Exod33:17)). Instead, he would send an angel ([33:2](https://ref.ly/Exod33:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 33:1-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Stubborn (ID: `keyterm:Stubborn`); Hittites (ID: `group:Hittite`); Amorites (ID: `group:Amorites`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Canaanites (ID: `group:Canaan`); Heth (ID: `person:Heth`); Mount Sinai (ID: `place:SinaiMount`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Land Flowing of Milk and Honey (ID: `keyterm:LandFlowingOfMilkAndHoney`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Perizzites (ID: `group:Perizzites`); Hivites (ID: `group:Hivites`); Jebusites (ID: `group:Jebusites`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)
## Exodus 33:2–3 (id: 11335)

* *an angel … But I will not go with you:* This *angel* might not be the "angel of the LORD," since in Exodus and other parts of the Old Testament, the angel of the LORD is closely associated with God's presence (see [3:2](https://ref.ly/Exod3:2); [23:20–23](https://ref.ly/Exod23:20-Exod23:23)).

    Or, God might have meant that while he would lead them through the angel ([33:2](https://ref.ly/Exod33:2)), he would not live among them in the tabernacle as planned ([33:3](https://ref.ly/Exod33:3)).

* *Canaanites … Jebusites:* See study note on 3:8.
